ARIZONA INSURANCE DEPARTMENT OF ARIZONA Business of 1962 G. A. BUSHNELL Director of Insurance

PREFACE TO FIFTIETH REPORT 

One of Arizona's truly dynamic industries is insurance. Arizona currently ranks second in the number (153) of home state (domestic) insurance companies; only Texas has more. These companies, together with 697 out-of-state (foreign) insurers, are represented in the state by slightly more than 11,000 agents, brokers and solicitors licensed by the 

Insurance Department. 

Moreover, growth in insurance activities is continuing at a spectacular pace, as measured by the experience of the last ten years. For example, life insurance in force has increased from $1 billion in 1952 to $5.7 billion in 1962. Obviously the economy of the state has been, and is continuing to be, vitally and affirmatively affected by this substantial activity. 

Regulation of insurance in the public interest emerged slowly, commencing in 1887, twenty-five years before statehood, when foreign insurers were required to pay an annual license fee of $200 to each county in which an office was maintained. By 1901 a systematic scheme of insurance laws had evolved, administered by the Secretary of the Territory, a federal appointee from Washington, D. C., with an office comparable to the present Secretary of State.

In 1913 our First Legislature placed insurance regulation within the Corporation Commission. Thereafter, insurance laws and the Director's authority were periodically reviewed and rendered stronger and more comprehensive. These early improvements took place most notably in 1915, 1928, 1933, 1943 and 1947. 

Then in 1954 the Twenty-first Legislature completely revised and codified all insurance laws and constituted the Insurance Department as a separate body of government altogether independent of the Corporation Commission except that the appointment of the Director of Insurance remains in its hands. In 1956 the Insurance Department moved its offices from the crowded Arizona State Building near the Capitol to its more spacious present location at 718 West Glenrosa, from where it more conveniently and adequately serves the industry and the public. In the years that have followed, the Department has grown commensurate with the expansion of the industry regulated. 

The 1954 code, drafted under the able direction of Seattle insurance attorney Robert Williams, modernized insurance regulation in Arizona and proved so beneficial that it since has served as a model by seven other states in recodifying insurance legislation of their own. Even so, the Insurance Department aided considerably by a cooperative committee involving insurance companies, agents and the general public thereafter continued to work periodically with legislators to draft new laws designed in the public interest to keep regulation abreast with rapidly evolving developments within the industry. 

The scope of insurance regulation in Arizona is broad imposing qualification requirements upon every insurer seeking to transact business in this state, authorizing periodic financial examinations and inspections of insurers, causing audits to be made of premium tax returns of all insurers, requiring the licensing of all agents and the disciplining of them as needed, directing the approval of policy forms and premium rates, and providing for the processing of policyholder complaints and the answering of inquiries from companies, agents, lawyers, legislators and the general public. 

Vigorous enforcement of laws and regulations in these areas has resulted in a quality atmosphere for marketing and purchasing insurance in Arizona. One dramatic evidence of this already noted is the rapid growth of basic insurance coverages. A further evidence is that, notwithstanding the great number of domestic companies which have been chartered, only seven have operated in such manner as to compel conservation or liquidation 

A new area of administration was added to Departmental responsibilities in 1962. 

To conform with successful experience in sister states and to fill a void in public safety regulation, the office of Commissioner of Public Buliding Safety was created and the Director named as its administrator. Since that time the various fire chiefs have been appointed deputy commissioners, inspections of hazardous facilities have been conducted and information has been dispensed relative to safety devices, first aid, location and adequacy of exits, and installation and maintenance of private fire protection equipment. 

The public service portion of the Department's responsibility has been particularly active. A recent sampling of the Department's operations in Phoenix shows that, on the average, each day 65 visitors are received, 170 telephone calls are answered and 425 items of correspondence are dispatched. It is a source of pleasure and satisfaction that included frequently among incoming letters are declarations of thanks and good will for effective assistance, helpful advice or courteous attention to problems.

The Insurance Department in net operations in no sense is a drain upon taxpayers. 

On the contrary, it is a substantial revenue producing agency and more so with each passing year. In 1913 the Insurance Department collected in fees and taxes for remission to the State Treasurer the sum of $41,837. In 1950-51 such annual collections passed the $1 million mark. Now, for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1963, the Department's collections and remissions totalled $4,922,637 an increase of 11,800 per cent in 50 years and of 4,900 percent in just the last 12 years. Less than six per cent of annual revenue is returned to the Department for the administration of its various responsibilities. 

Fifty years of successful regulation of insurance in Arizona has bestowed great benefit upon the citizens of the state and upon our economy. The Insurance Department dedicates itself anew to the continuing fulfillment of its on-going responsibilities.

G. A. BUSHNELL 

Director of Insurance

PERSONNEL OF THE INSURANCE DEPARTMENT 

June 30, 1963 Arizona law prescribes comprehensive insurance regulation in the public interest. 

Such requires a continually active supervision and maintenance of timely and accurate records. Consequently, the Insurance Department has employed and trained a staff of specialists together with competent assistants and clerks to perform essential tasks. As the state continues to grow and to broaden the areas of insurance regulation, the Department too must expand. On June 30, 1963, Departmental officers and employees were:

ACCOMPLISHMENTS AND FUNCTIONS OF THE INSURANCE DEPARTMENT 

1962 The Insurance Department is charged with the duty and responsibility of administering Arizona Revised Statutes, Title 20, consisting of 331 pages of detailed insurance laws. The provisions thereof cover more areas of regulation and supervision than can be beneficially summarized here. But the Department's principal accomplishments and functions during the 

1962 calendar year and the 1962-1963 fiscal year were as follows: 

COLLECTION OF TAXES, FEES AND LICENSES 

Premium taxes, together with fees and licenses, collected and remitted to the State Treasurer for the fiscal year amounted to $4,922,637.05. Such constituted an increase of $477,423.07 over collections during the preceding year. 

AUDITS OF INSURER'S ANNUAL STATEMENTS 

Office audits were made of annual statements of all 850 insurers authorized to transact insurance in Arizona. Such audits embraced careful verification of premium tax returns and of financial and operating procedures of every authorized insurer. 

PROCESSING OF INSURER'S APPLICATIONS FOR ADMISSION 

Applications of 117 insurers seeking original authority to transact insurance in Arizona were inspected and reviewed. Based thereon, 63 new authorizations were issued. 

CONDUCT OF FIELD EXAMINATIONS 

Comprehensive field examinations of 61 domestic insurers were conducted by trained and experienced examiners. Every examination was administered in the manner prescribed by the "Manual of Examination - Practice and Procedure", a comprehensive guide prepared by the National Association of Insurance Commissioners. 

PROCESSING OF COMPLAINTS AND INQUIRIES 

The investigation and servicing of public complaints against insurance companies, agents, brokers, solicitors and bail bondsmen constitute a vital function of the Insurance Department. Complaints processed numbered 684. The Department also responded to a large volume of inquiries and communications received both in Phoenix and Tucson by personal visit (70 visitors daily), by telephone (185 calls daily) and by mail (approximately 

325 items daily).

LICENSING OF AGENTS, BROKERS AND SOLICITORS 

Each person desiring to transact insurance as an agent, broker or solicitor must apply for license and be examined. Written examinations were prepared for approximately 2100 individuals for administration on scheduled occasions in Phoenix, Tucson, Yuma and Flagstaff. Based thereon, 1555 licenses were issued.

APPROVAL OF FORMS AND FILING OF RATES 

Every life and disability insurer must file with the Director and obtain approval of each policy, rider, endorsement and application form issued in Arizona and every property and casualty insurer must file each rate schedule used. Approximately 7200 life and disability forms were reviewed and 2400 fire and casualty rate schedules filed. 

INSURANCE LEGISLATION ADOPTED IN 1963 

The First Session of the 26th Legislature in 1963 passed three laws of general interest relative to insurance and its regulation. First, the new uniform industrial mortality tables were adopted. Second, up to $10,000 in cash values of life policies that name family members as beneficiaries were rendered exempt from creditors. Third, insurance transactions of motor clubs were made subject to insurance regulation. 

Undoubtedly, in the years ahead, the public interest will call for added legislation to cope with new developments and changing circumstances affecting a rapidly expanding industry. The Insurance Department will continue to take the lead in the promotion of such legislation as circumstances prescribe.

LIQUIDATION AND DISSOLUTION OF ARIZONA INSURERS 

1937 - 1963 Even though Arizona is the home of more insurance corporations than any other state excepting one, the number of domestic insurers that have failed, thus requiring liquidation and eventual dissolution, is among the lowest in the country only seven during the last twenty-six years. Liquidations are commenced by the Director upon application to the Maricopa County Superior Court which then appoints the Director as receiver, serving under the court's supervision. The seven companies subjected to such proceedings were:

UNION RESERVE LIFE INSURANCE COMPANY (1938) 

On March 3, 1938, Union Reserve Life Insurance Company, a legal reserve life insurer, was placed in liquidation. The company's assets consisted mostly of mortgages of grossly inflated value. On August, 20, 1945, the company was dissolved and the receivership closed.

TRANS-PACIFIC INSURANCE COMPANY (1955) 

On September 7, 1955, Trans-Pacific Insurance Company, a legal reserve casualty insurer, was placed in liquidation. Proceedings were slowed considerably by contested claims and complicated litigation. Finally, however, on December 28, 1962 the company was dissolved and the receivership closed. 

NATIONAL SECURITY LIFE INSURANCE COMPANY (1956) On November 8, 1956, National Security Life Insurance Company, a limited capital stock insurer, was placed in liquidation. Assets were quickly marshalled and no stockholders' assessment was necessary. All claims approved by the court were paid one hundred cents on the dollar, and on May 8, 1957 the company was dissolved and the receivership closed.

INSURED SAVINGS INSURANCE COMPANY (1956) 

On November 30, 1956, Insured Savings Insurance Company, a mutual benefit insurer, was placed in liquidation. Approved claims were paid ninety cents on the dollar, and on August 16, 1957 the company was dissolved and the receivership closed. 

PHOENIX LIFE INSURANCE, INC. (1960) 

On July 26, 1960, Phoenix Life Insurance, Inc., a limited capital stock insurer, was placed in liquidation. Assets were quickly marshalled and no stockholders' assessment was necessary. All claims approved by the court were paid one hundred cents on the dollar, and on June 1, 1961 the company was dissolved and the receivership closed. 

COSMOPOLITAN LIFE INSURANCE COMPANY (1962) 

On May 22, 1962, Cosmopolitan Life Insurance Company, a mutual benefit insurer, was placed in liquidation. Assets were quickly marshalled and all creditors paid one hundred cents on the dollar. Dissolution of the company and closing of the receivership are pending.

PACIFIC SOUTHWEST LIFE INSURANCE COMPANY (1963) 

On April 22, 1963, Pacific Southwest Life Insurance Company, a legal reserve life insurer, was placed in liquidation. Presently assets and liabilities are being determined, and every effort is being extended to minimize losses and to expedite proceedings.

REPORT OF OFFICE OF COMMISSIONER OF PUBLIC BUILDING SAFETY 

There follows a brief outline of activities of the Commissioner of Public Building 

Safety since June, 1962, which was the effective date of legislation that created such office under the Director of Insurance. A.R.S. Sections 20-1701 through 20-1709 set forth the duties and functions of the Commissioner and his office. 

The fire chiefs of all organized fire districts have been appointed deputy commissioners and have been furnished a copy of applicable law. These men have been encouraged to contact the Department for information and assistance when needed. 

A membership in the National Fire Protection Association has been effected to assist in performance of important functions. The Association distributes to members data on modern methods of fire safety and circulates pamphlets on safety devices and on fire protection and electrical equipment. Its publications have served as invaluable guides to fire chiefs and others interested in fire protection and safety throughout the United States and Canada. 

The office has received many requests for information from deputy commissioners and from private citizens. Most of these have been relative to the installation and maintenance of private fire protection equipment, standard construction of motion picture projection booths and location and adequacy of exits. Questions have been answered and standard specifications, diagrams and other data have been supplied to assist in solving their problems. 

When requested, special trips have been made to the fire protection districts to attend meetings or to conduct inspections. These activities are detailed in the paragraphs that follow. 

1. December 28, 1962. Fire Chief C. C. Webster of Gilbert had requested that a trip be made to his town for the purpose of conducting two inspections. 

The first inspection was of a gas station where a perilous situation existed. In consequence of the visit, the proprietor cleared a trash pile and rearranged used tires so as to render the premises and adjoining property relatively safe from fire hazard. 

The second inspection was of a cotton seed and safflower oil mill and was conducted along with the plant superintendent. The visit proved rewarding. The principal hazards found were small fires allowed in rubbish and seed piles in the plant yard, which were a danger not only to the plant but also to adjacent property. The plant superintendent agreed to segregate an area for burning and to eliminate spot fires. Also eliminated were two or three minor hazards. 

2. January 8, 1963. A trip was made to Casa Grande for the purpose of inspecting the 

National Guard Armory, accompanied by Fire Chief N. A. Coxon. The Armory is located outside the city limits, about one-half mile east of Highway 187 on the northside of Cottonwood Lane. The question was whether an 8" public water main on the southside of the street, apposite the Armory, was sufficient fire protection for this property. The officer in charge advised that the National Guard could not finance installation of a hydrant plus a $5.00 monthly standby charge. Nevertheless, it was recommended that such a hydrant be installed. 

3. March 12 and 13, 1963. Fire Chief J. C. Custer of Prescott had requested a trip be made to conduct an inspection of schools and particularly to review plans and specifications of a new high school. Two meetings were held involving the superintendent of schools, school board members, the architect and Chief Custer. A full discussion was held concerning adequacy of exits, installation of first aid fire protection equipment and safety principles applicable to life and property. It was concluded that an additional exit would be installed for the safety of occupants. 

4. May 8 and 9, 1963. A trip was made to Nogales to attend the annual meeting of the 

League of Arizona Cities and Towns, fire chiefs division. Included was a session with fire chiefs on laws governing public building safety. The meeting was satisfactory, but unfortunately only a few chiefs were in attendance. 

5. The State Department of Health requested attendance at a hearing relative to nursing and sheltered care homes. The meeting was with Ralph H. Conway, Assistant Director of 

Hospitals and Medical Facilities. Advice was given relative to exits and to proper installation of standard first aid and of fire protection equipment, and afterwards Mr. Conway was furnished additional information relative to fire protection, exits and safety of occupants in buildings under his jurisdiction. Plans and specifications for the proposed Cottonwood Hospital were also considered resulting in recommendations on exits, protection against fire hazards, installation of an automatic sprinkler system and use of first aid. We are informed that a supervising federal agency has acceded to these recommendations. Finally, plans and specifications for other buildings, either proposed or completed, were reviewed with Mr. Conway and safety suggestions made.

G. A. BUSHNELL Commissioner of Public Building Safety By: C. W. Lord Chief Deputy Commissioner

REPORT ON COLLECTIONS FOR FIREMEN'S RELIEF AND PENSION FUND 

1962 A. R. S. Section 20-224, by reference to A. R. S. Sections 9-951 and 9-952, provides that net premium taxes collected each year by the Director on fire insurance shall be apportioned to each town or district having a fire company in accordance with premiums received on properties therein and supplemented by a pro rata share of premium taxes collected on properties outside all such towns or districts and provides further that all such sums shall be remitted by the Director to the State Treasurer for deposit in the firemen's relief and pension fund. In 1962 such taxes totalled $347,603.94, which was $44,382.17 more than the $303,221.77 collected in 1961. The 1962 taxes were duly apportioned as follows:

REPORT ON COLLECTIONS FOR HIGHWAY PATROL RETIREMENT FUND 

1962 A.R.S. Section 20-224.01 provides that, beginning on July 1, 1959, every foreign or alien insurer each year shall pay to the Director for remission to the State Treasurer a tax of 1/2 of 1 per cent of net premiums on vehicle insurance and provides further that such sum shall be appropriated to the state retirement system board for deposit in the state highway patrol retirement fund. The 1962 collections totalled $270,462.13, which was $26,031.13 more than the $244,431.00 collected in 1961.
